--- NODE c12_comm_013 ---
TITLE: Types of Plans — Objectives, Strategy, Policy, Procedures, Method, Rule, Budget, Program
DEFINITION: |
  Planning, as established in the previous node (c12_comm_012), is the process of setting objectives and choosing the course of action to achieve them. The output of that process is not a single uniform document but eight distinct types of plans, each serving a different level of specificity and time horizon. These eight types — objectives, strategy, policy, procedures, method, rule, budget, and program — together form a hierarchy from the most general and long-range to the most specific and short-range, and they are also classified as either standing plans (reusable across time) or single-use plans (designed for one specific situation).

  Objectives are the end goals that an organisation seeks to achieve. They are the most general and abstract type of plan because they specify "what" is to be accomplished but not "how". Tata Steel's objective — "to be the world's best steel company" — states an aspiration without prescribing the means. Objectives must be specific enough to be measurable (Tata Steel's 2030 objective includes producing 30 million tonnes of crude steel annually) yet broad enough to guide the entire organisation. Under Section 134(3)(f) of the Companies Act 2013, the Board's report must disclose the annual objectives and key performance indicators, making objectives a legally documented plan type for every Indian company.

  Strategy is the comprehensive approach chosen to achieve the organisation's objectives in the face of competition and resource constraints. Strategy answers "how, broadly, will we reach the objective?" When Tata Steel acquired Corus in 2007 for £6.2 billion (approximately ₹60,000 crore at then exchange rates), the acquisition was a strategic decision — the chosen approach to become a global steel major by gaining European manufacturing capacity and access to developed markets. Strategy involves allocating resources, identifying competitive advantages, and positioning the firm relative to rivals. It is more specific than an objective but broader than a policy.

  Policy is a flexible guideline that channels managerial decisions within defined boundaries. A policy does not dictate a specific action; it sets the limits within which decisions can be made. Tata Steel's policy "promote local hiring in Jamshedpur" does not say "hire person X" — it tells the HR department that, when filling a vacancy, preference should be given to local candidates unless a specialised skill is unavailable locally. Policies are standing plans: they are used repeatedly across many decisions over time. Section 135 of the Companies Act 2013, read with the CSR Rules, mandates that every company with net worth of ₹500 crore or more, or turnover of ₹1,000 crore or more, or net profit of ₹5 crore or more, must formulate a CSR policy and spend at least 2% of its average net profit of the preceding three years on CSR activities. This makes the CSR policy a legally required plan type — a policy mandated by statute, not merely by managerial choice.

  Procedures are sequences of steps required to complete a recurring task. A procedure specifies the order in which actions must be taken. Tata Steel's seven-step recruitment process for plant operators — (1) receive manpower requisition, (2) screen applications, (3) conduct written aptitude test, (4) hold technical interview, (5) medical examination, (6) background verification, (7) issue appointment letter — is a procedure. A procedure is more specific than a policy because it prescribes the exact sequence, not merely the boundary. Procedures are standing plans because the same sequence is followed every time a plant operator is hired.

  Method is the specific technique used to perform one step within a procedure. Whereas a procedure is a sequence of many steps, a method is the prescribed way of doing one of those steps. Tata Steel specifies a particular welding technique (submerged arc welding) for joining high-tensile steel plates of a particular grade — this is a method. A procedure contains multiple methods: step 4 of the recruitment procedure (technical interview) might use the method of "structured behavioural interviewing using the STAR framework". Methods are standing plans because the same technique is prescribed each time that step is performed. The common student error is to treat procedure and method as interchangeable — a procedure is a multi-step sequence, a method is a single technique within that sequence.

  Rule is the most rigid type of plan — a non-negotiable statement of what must or must not be done, with no managerial discretion. Tata Steel's rule "no worker enters the blast furnace area without safety gear" permits no exception; a worker either complies or faces disciplinary action. Rules differ from policies because policies allow discretion within boundaries, whereas rules allow none. The Tata Code of Conduct, adopted in 1998 and revised periodically, is a set of rules — "no bribery", "no child labour", "no discrimination" — that apply across all Tata companies and admit of no exception. Ethical rules encode the moral floor below which the organisation will not operate.

  Budget is a single-use plan that states, in numerical (usually monetary) terms, the expected results and the resources allocated to achieve them, for a specific period. Tata Steel's capital expenditure budget of ₹2,000 crore for FY 2023-24 specifies how much will be spent on new facilities, modernisation, and capacity expansion during that financial year. A budget is single-use because it applies to one specific period; next year's budget is a new plan. Budgets may be prepared for the organisation as a whole or for each department, function, or project.

  Program is a single-use plan that coordinates a set of activities to achieve a specific objective within a defined timeframe. Tata Steel's "Green Steel Initiative" — a program to reduce carbon emissions by 30% by 2030 through hydrogen-based steelmaking, renewable energy sourcing, and carbon capture — is a program. A program is broader than a budget (it includes activities, timelines, responsibilities, and budgets) and is single-use because it is designed for one specific objective and timeframe. The Reserve Bank of India sets standing plans (monetary policy, credit policy) and rules (CRR and SLR ratios under Sections 18 and 24 of the Banking Regulation Act 1949) that every Indian bank — SBI, HDFC, ICICI — must incorporate into its own planning hierarchy, illustrating how institutional rules cascade down to firm-level plans.
TEXT_WALKTHROUGH: |
  In the previous node (c12_comm_012) you learned that planning is the process of setting objectives, identifying alternatives, evaluating them, and selecting the best. But the output of that process is not one document — it is eight different types of plans, each operating at a different level of specificity. This node unpacks those eight types and shows how they fit together as a hierarchy.

  Let us anchor this in a single Indian mission that used all eight plan types — ISRO's Chandrayaan-3, launched on 14 July 2023 and successfully soft-landed on the Moon's south pole on 23 August 2023. Ask yourself: what was the objective? To soft-land a spacecraft on the lunar south pole — a region no country had reached before. That is the most general plan: it states the end goal. How did ISRO plan to achieve it? The strategy was a cost-effective mission at ₹615 crore (compared to NASA's comparable missions costing several thousand crore), using a proven LVM-3 launch vehicle and an indigenous lander-rover configuration. Strategy is the broad approach. What guideline shaped ISRO's decisions throughout? The policy was "use indigenously built components to the maximum extent possible" — a flexible guideline that channelled procurement decisions without dictating each one. What sequence of steps did the mission follow? The procedure included the multi-phase launch, Earth-bound manoeuvres, trans-lunar injection, lunar-bound manoeuvres, and powered descent — a defined sequence. What specific technique was used for the critical final descent? The method was the powered descent algorithm that throttled the lander's engines to achieve a soft touchdown — a single technique within the descent procedure. What rigid constraint applied? The rule was "no launch window extension beyond July 2023" because the lunar geometry would not permit a landing thereafter — a non-negotiable constraint. What was the financial plan? The budget was ₹615 crore, allocated across launch vehicle, spacecraft, ground systems, and operations. What coordinated the whole effort? The program was the Chandrayaan-3 mission plan — a single-use plan that integrated objectives, strategy, policies, procedures, methods, rules, and budget into one coordinated effort with a defined timeline.

  Now arrange these eight types in order from most general to most specific: Objectives → Strategy → Policy → Procedures → Method → Rule. Notice that Budget and Program sit slightly outside this hierarchy because they are single-use plans (one-time), whereas the other six are standing plans (reusable). A standing plan like SBI's loan policy is applied to thousands of loan decisions over many years; a single-use plan like the IPL 2024 event budget applies to one tournament in one year.

  Here is the common error that costs marks. Students confuse "procedure" with "method". A procedure is a sequence of multiple steps to complete a task — ISRO's launch-to-landing procedure has perhaps twenty steps. A method is the specific technique used in one of those steps — the powered descent algorithm is one method within that procedure. Ask yourself: "Is this a multi-step sequence, or a single technique?" If multi-step sequence → procedure. If single technique → method. A 7-step procurement process is a procedure; the specific technique of evaluating suppliers by weighted scoring is a method within step 3 of that procedure.

  Another distinction that examiners test: policy versus rule. A policy is flexible — "prefer local hiring in Jamshedpur" allows the HR manager to hire a non-local candidate if a specialised skill is unavailable locally. A rule is rigid — "no entry to the blast furnace area without safety gear" allows no exception. The test: "Can a manager make an exception in a justified case?" If yes → policy. If no → rule.

  This node links to your Class 12 Mathematics work on linear programming (c12_math_085). In linear programming, you set an objective function (maximise profit), identify constraints (limited machine hours, raw material), and find the optimal solution. The "objective function" corresponds to the "objectives" plan type; the "constraints" correspond to "policies" and "rules" that bound managerial action. Managerial planning is linear programming at a qualitative level — set the goal, identify the boundaries, choose the path.

  The key takeaway: the eight plan types form a hierarchy from general (objectives) to specific (rule), with budget and program as single-use plans that cut across the hierarchy. Master the procedure-vs-method and policy-vs-rule distinctions, and you will answer any classification question correctly.
ELI5: |
  Imagine your family is planning a wedding. The objective is "a memorable wedding". The strategy is "host it in Goa to combine wedding and holiday". The policy is "prefer vegetarian caterers" (flexible — non-veg allowed if guests insist). The procedure is the 10-step sequence: book venue, send invites, book caterer, book decorator, and so on. The method is the specific way the caterer cooks biryani (dum method). The rule is "no alcohol served to minors" (no exceptions). The budget is ₹15 lakh for the whole event. The program is the 3-month wedding plan that ties everything together. Eight plans, one wedding.
COMMON_MISCONCEPTION: |
  Misconception 1: "Procedure and method are the same thing — both are about how to do a task." Correction: A procedure is a sequence of multiple steps to complete a task; a method is the specific technique used in one of those steps. Tata Steel's 7-step recruitment process is a procedure (seven steps in order). The structured behavioural interviewing technique used in step 4 (the technical interview) is a method (one technique within one step). A procedure contains multiple methods; a method is a single technique. Ask: "Is this a multi-step sequence or a single technique?" If multi-step → procedure; if single technique → method.

  Misconception 2: "Policy and rule are both guidelines, so they are interchangeable." Correction: A policy is flexible — it allows managerial discretion within boundaries. A rule is rigid — it admits no exception. Tata Steel's policy "prefer local hiring in Jamshedpur" allows the HR manager to hire a non-local candidate if a specialised skill is unavailable locally. Tata Steel's rule "no worker enters the blast furnace area without safety gear" allows no exception — a worker without gear is sent back, full stop. The test: "Can a manager make a justified exception?" If yes → policy; if no → rule.

  Misconception 3: "Budget and program are standing plans because every company has them every year." Correction: A standing plan is one that is used repeatedly over time without being recreated (policy, procedure, method, rule). A budget is single-use because each year's budget is a new plan — Tata Steel's ₹2,000 crore capex budget for FY 2023-24 expires on 31 March 2024 and a new budget is created for FY 2024-25. A program is single-use because it is designed for one specific objective and timeframe — the Green Steel Initiative ends when its 2030 carbon-reduction target is met. The fact that a company makes a new budget each year does not make "budget" a standing plan; each instance is a fresh single-use plan.
DIAGNOSTIC_QUESTION: |
  Q1 (recall): Define all eight types of plans and give one Indian example of each. Arrange the six standing-plan types in order from most general to most specific.
  Answer: (1) Objectives — the end goals; example: Tata Steel's objective "to be the world's best steel company". (2) Strategy — the comprehensive approach to achieve objectives; example: Tata Steel's acquisition of Corus in 2007 for £6.2 billion to gain European market access. (3) Policy — a flexible guideline for decisions; example: Tata Steel's policy "promote local hiring in Jamshedpur". (4) Procedures — a sequence of steps to complete a recurring task; example: Tata Steel's 7-step recruitment process for plant operators. (5) Method — a specific technique for performing one step; example: submerged arc welding for joining high-tensile steel plates. (6) Rule — a rigid, non-negotiable must/must-not; example: "no worker enters the blast furnace area without safety gear". (7) Budget — a single-use numerical plan of resources and expected results for a period; example: Tata Steel's ₹2,000 crore capital expenditure budget for FY 2023-24. (8) Program — a single-use coordinated set of activities for a specific objective and timeframe; example: Tata Steel's "Green Steel Initiative" to reduce carbon emissions by 30% by 2030. The six standing-plan types in order from most general to most specific: Objectives → Strategy → Policy → Procedures → Method → Rule.

  Q2 (understand): Distinguish between a "policy" and a "rule" with one example of each from an Indian bank. Then classify each of the following three statements as one of the eight plan types, with justification: (a) "SBI shall maintain a Cash Reserve Ratio of 4.5% of its Net Demand and Time Liabilities as prescribed by RBI." (b) "HDFC Bank prefers to lend to borrowers with a CIBIL score above 750." (c) "ICICI Bank's 8-step loan disbursement process: application → CIBIL check → income verification → KYC → sanction letter → property valuation (for home loans) → disbursement → post-disbursement monitoring."
  Answer: A policy is a flexible guideline that channels decisions within boundaries, allowing managerial discretion. A rule is a rigid, non-negotiable statement that admits no exception. Example of a policy: HDFC Bank's guideline "prefer borrowers with CIBIL score above 750" — a loan officer may still approve a borrower with a score of 720 if the borrower has a strong income profile and collateral, so discretion exists. Example of a rule: SBI must maintain CRR at 4.5% of NDTL as prescribed by RBI under Section 18 of the Banking Regulation Act 1949 — no branch manager can choose to maintain less; non-compliance attracts penalty. Classification: (a) Rule — it is a rigid statutory requirement with no discretion; the CRR ratio is fixed by RBI and SBI must comply exactly. (b) Policy — it is a flexible guideline; the word "prefers" signals that exceptions are possible at the loan officer's discretion. (c) Procedure — it is a defined sequence of eight steps to be followed in order every time a loan is disbursed; it is a multi-step sequence (not a single technique, so not a method; not a flexible guideline, so not a policy).

  Q3 (apply): When HDFC Bank planned its merger with HDFC Ltd (announced April 2022, completed July 2023), it used all eight plan types. Identify each of the eight plan types in this merger with a specific statement, and classify each as standing plan or single-use plan.
  Answer: (1) Objective: "Create India's largest private-sector bank by combining HDFC Bank's banking platform with HDFC Ltd's housing loan portfolio" — a standing plan (the end goal persists). (2) Strategy: "Merge the parent (HDFC Ltd) into the subsidiary (HDFC Bank) through a share-swap arrangement approved by RBI and NCLT" — the broad approach. (3) Policy: "Retain all existing employees of HDFC Ltd post-merger, redeploying them within the combined entity" — a flexible guideline allowing case-by-case redeployment decisions. (4) Procedure: "Obtain RBI approval → obtain NCLT approval → obtain shareholder approval → obtain CCI approval → fix record date → allot shares → complete transfer of assets and liabilities" — a multi-step sequence. (5) Method: "Use the share-swap ratio of 42 HDFC Bank shares for every 25 HDFC Ltd shares, calculated using the relative net worth and market price method" — a specific valuation technique. (6) Rule: "No HDFC Bank branch shall be closed for 12 months post-merger, per RBI's conditional approval" — a rigid, non-negotiable condition. (7) Budget: "₹12,000 crore earmarked for integration costs including IT system merger, branch rebranding, and employee training" — a single-use numerical plan for the integration period. (8) Program: "The 18-month integration program from April 2022 to October 2023 covering legal, operational, IT, HR, and customer migration activities" — a single-use coordinated set of activities for a specific objective and timeframe. Standing plans: objective, strategy, policy, procedure, method, rule. Single-use plans: budget, program.

  Q4 (analyze/evaluate): "The eight types of plans form a strict hierarchy from most general to most specific, and every managerial decision must flow down this hierarchy in order." Evaluate this statement. Is the hierarchy strict? Must decisions always flow in order? Use the Tata Steel and ISRO Chandrayaan-3 examples to support your analysis.
  Answer: The statement is partially correct but overstated. The hierarchy from objectives → strategy → policy → procedures → method → rule does represent a general-to-specific ordering, and in well-designed planning systems, higher-level plans do constrain lower-level plans — Tata Steel's objective ("best steel company") shapes its strategy (acquire Corus), which shapes its policy (local hiring to support community relations post-acquisition), which shapes its procedures (7-step recruitment), and so on. This top-down cascade is real and important. However, the hierarchy is not strictly linear in two respects. First, budget and program are single-use plans that cut across the hierarchy — ISRO's Chandrayaan-3 program integrated objectives, strategy, policy, procedure, method, rule, and budget into one coordinated effort, rather than sitting neatly at one level. Second, planning in practice is often iterative, not purely top-down. When ISRO encountered the launch-window constraint (the rule that no extension beyond July 2023 was possible), this rigid rule actually shaped the strategy and the procedure — the team had to compress the mission timeline to fit the window. So lower-level plans (rules, constraints) can feed back upward and reshape higher-level plans. The hierarchy is best understood as a logical ordering (general to specific) and a default flow (top-down), but not as a rigid sequence that forbids feedback or cross-cutting single-use plans. The correct evaluation: the hierarchy is a useful organising principle, but real planning is iterative and integrates standing and single-use plans in a web, not a straight line.

  Q5 (create): Construct a classification chart (in text form) that a Class 12 student can use to classify any given company statement as one of the eight plan types. Your chart must use at least four decision questions, each answered yes/no, leading to the correct classification. Test your chart on two statements: (a) "Infosys shall spend ₹400 crore on employee training in FY 2024-25." (b) "All Infosys employees must complete mandatory cybersecurity training by 31 March 2025; non-completion will result in lock-out of email access."
  Answer: Classification Chart:
    Step 1: Is the statement a one-time plan for a specific period or specific objective (not reusable next year)?
      → If YES → Single-use plan. Go to Step 2.
      → If NO → Standing plan. Go to Step 3.
    Step 2 (Single-use): Is the statement primarily a numerical/monetary allocation of resources for a period?
      → If YES → BUDGET.
      → If NO (it is a coordinated set of activities with a timeline and objective) → PROGRAM.
    Step 3 (Standing plan): Is the statement an end goal (the "what") without specifying the means?
      → If YES → OBJECTIVE.
      → If NO → Go to Step 4.
    Step 4: Is the statement a broad approach to achieving the objective (the "how, broadly")?
      → If YES → STRATEGY.
      → If NO → Go to Step 5.
    Step 5: Is the statement a flexible guideline that allows managerial discretion within boundaries?
      → If YES → POLICY.
      → If NO → Go to Step 6.
    Step 6: Is the statement a multi-step sequence to complete a recurring task?
      → If YES → PROCEDURE.
      → If NO → Go to Step 7.
    Step 7: Is the statement a single specific technique for performing one step?
      → If YES → METHOD.
      → If NO (it is a rigid must/must-not with no discretion) → RULE.

    Test (a): "Infosys shall spend ₹400 crore on employee training in FY 2024-25."
      Step 1: One-time plan for a specific period? YES → Single-use. Step 2: Primarily a numerical/monetary allocation? YES → BUDGET. ✓ Correct.

    Test (b): "All Infosys employees must complete mandatory cybersecurity training by 31 March 2025; non-completion will result in lock-out of email access."
      Step 1: One-time plan for a specific period? The training is for FY 2024-25, but the requirement "all employees must complete" is a rigid mandate. Consider the dominant character: the statement is a non-negotiable must-do with a stated consequence. Step 3: End goal? NO. Step 4: Broad approach? NO. Step 5: Flexible guideline allowing discretion? NO (it says "must complete" with a penalty). Step 6: Multi-step sequence? NO. Step 7: Single technique? NO → RULE. ✓ Correct — this is a rule because it admits no exception and prescribes a consequence for non-compliance.
ON_FAILURE: |
  Q1 fails -> Review DEPTH_PREREQ node c12_math_085 (linear programming) to recall how an objective function and constraints structure a problem, then re-read the DEFINITION paragraphs on each of the eight plan types and the COMPARATIVE_FRAMEWORK hierarchy. The gap is recall of all eight types and their ordering. After review, retry Q1.

  Q2 fails -> Review PREREQ node c12_comm_012 (planning meaning and steps) to recall that planning produces specific outputs, then re-read the DEFINITION paragraphs on "policy" and "rule" and the TEXT_WALKTHROUGH paragraph on the policy-vs-rule distinction. The gap is the flexibility-vs-rigidity test. After review, retry Q2.

  Q3 fails -> Review the DEFINITION paragraphs on all eight plan types and the DECISION_CONTEXT field in the metadata (HDFC-HDFC Ltd merger), then re-read the TEXT_WALKTHROUGH's ISRO Chandrayaan-3 example which maps all eight types onto one mission.   Q4 fails -> Review the COMPARATIVE_FRAMEWORK field and the DEFINITION paragraphs on standing vs single-use plans, then re-read the final paragraph of the TEXT_WALKTHROUGH on the hierarchy and the link to linear programming. MASTERY_CRITERIA: |
  Can define all eight types of plans (objectives, strategy, policy, procedures, method, rule, budget, program) with one Indian example each; can arrange them in order from most general to most specific (objectives → strategy → policy → procedures → method → rule); can distinguish a "policy" (flexible guideline) from a "rule" (rigid, non-negotiable) with a correct example; can distinguish a "procedure" (multi-step sequence) from a "method" (single technique) with a correct example; can classify any given company statement as one of the eight plan types with justification; can identify standing plans (policy, procedure, method, rule) versus single-use plans (budget, program).

  Teach: meaning and features of all eight plan types (objectives, strategy, policy, procedures, method, rule, budget, program) with Indian examples; the hierarchy from most general to most specific; the standing-plan vs single-use-plan classification; the policy-vs-rule and procedure-vs-method distinctions; the legal requirement of CSR policy under Section 135 of the Companies Act 2013.
  Do NOT teach: strategic management models such as SWOT, PEST, Porter's Five Forces (not in this syllabus); budgeting techniques such as zero-based budgeting or incremental budgeting (not in this syllabus); detailed program management or project management methodologies (not in this syllabus).
  Already covered: c12_comm_012 (planning — meaning, steps, features) established what planning is and the steps of the planning process, of which the eight plan types are specific outputs.


--- NODE c12_comm_014 ---
TITLE: Organising — Meaning, Importance, Steps
DEFINITION: |
  Organising is the management function that follows planning in the management process. It is defined as the process of identifying and grouping activities to be performed, assigning duties to individuals and departments, and establishing authority and reporting relationships among them, so that the organisation's objectives — set during planning — can be achieved through coordinated human effort. If planning answers "what do we want to achieve and how?", organising answers "who will do what, in what groups, and reporting to whom?".

  The importance of organising rests on five benefits that are examined at ISC board level. First, organising enables benefits of specialisation: when work is divided into narrow, well-defined tasks and assigned to individuals who perform them repeatedly, those individuals develop expertise. Tata Consultancy Services (TCS) organises its 600,000+ employees into industry verticals — BFSI (Banking, Financial Services, Insurance), Retail, Manufacturing, Healthcare — so that engineers working in the BFSI vertical develop deep domain expertise in banking systems, making TCS the world's second-largest IT services company by market capitalisation as of 2023. Second, organising provides clarity of roles: every employee knows what they are responsible for and to whom they report, reducing overlap and confusion. Third, organising enables effective administration: a well-organised structure allows top management to focus on strategy while middle and supervisory management handle operations, creating a manageable span of control at each level. Fourth, organising supports growth and expansion: when Reliance Industries launched Jio in 2016, it created a separate Jio division with its own sales, network, and customer service departments, allowing Jio to scale to 450 million subscribers by 2023 without disrupting Reliance's other businesses. Fifth, organising promotes optimum utilisation of resources: by grouping related activities, the organisation avoids duplication of equipment, personnel, and effort.

  The organising process consists of five steps, each of which must be understood with an Indian example.

  Step 1 — Identification and Division of Work: The manager identifies the total work required to achieve the objectives and divides it into manageable activities. When Reliance launched Jio in 2016, it identified the key activities needed to run a telecom business: building and maintaining the network, selling SIM cards and plans, providing customer service, and managing billing. This is the identification of work. Division of work means breaking each of these into smaller tasks — network activity is divided into tower installation, fibre laying, spectrum management, and network monitoring.

  Step 2 — Departmentalisation: The divided activities are grouped into departments on the basis of function, product, territory, or customer. Jio's activities were grouped into departments: Jio Network (engineering), Jio Sales (marketing and distribution), Jio Care (customer service), and Jio Digital (apps and platforms). Departmentalisation brings together related activities under one manager, enabling coordination within the department.

  Step 3 — Assignment of Duties: Each department's tasks are assigned to specific individuals, defining their responsibility (the obligation to perform the assigned task) and granting them the authority (the right to command the resources and subordinates needed to perform it). Under Section 196 and Section 203 of the Companies Act 2013, every listed company and certain other companies must appoint Key Managerial Personnel — Chief Executive Officer, Chief Financial Officer, and Company Secretary — making the assignment of duties at the top level a legal requirement, not merely a managerial choice. The Ministry of Corporate Affairs requires every company to file its KMP appointments and changes through Form DIR-12, making the organising function legally documented and transparent.

  Step 4 — Establishing Reporting Relationships: Authority and reporting relationships are defined so that every employee knows to whom they report and who reports to them. The head of Jio Care reports to the Jio CEO; the Jio CEO reports to the Reliance Industries board. Without clear reporting relationships, accountability cannot be enforced.

  Step 5 — Coordination of Activities: The various departments and individuals must be coordinated so that their efforts converge on the organisation's objectives. Reliance holds weekly cross-departmental reviews where Jio Network, Jio Sales, and Jio Care heads align on network expansion plans, sales targets, and customer complaint trends. Coordination is the glue that holds the organised structure together.

  Three terms that students routinely confuse must be distinguished precisely. Authority is the right of a manager to command subordinates and deploy resources — it flows downward from superior to subordinate. Responsibility is the obligation of the subordinate to perform the assigned task — it also flows downward but is accepted by the subordinate (a subordinate can be assigned responsibility but must accept it for it to be effective). Accountability is the answerability of the subordinate for the results of the assigned task — it flows upward, because the subordinate is answerable to the superior for outcomes. When the Jio Sales head is given the authority to deploy a ₹500 crore marketing budget, the responsibility to achieve 50 million new subscriber additions, and is accountable to the Jio CEO for the results — all three concepts operate together but in different directions.
TEXT_WALKTHROUGH: |
  In the last two nodes you learned that planning sets objectives and produces eight types of plans. But a plan on paper achieves nothing until people are assigned to execute it. Organising is the function that turns a plan into a structure of human action. Let us build this step by step through a real Indian story.

  When Flipkart was acquired by Walmart in 2018 for $16 billion (approximately ₹1.15 lakh crore), Flipkart did not stay as one company. It had to re-organise. Why? Because Walmart now owned a complex portfolio — Flipkart (e-commerce), Myntra (fashion), and PhonePe (digital payments) — each with different customers, different competitors, and different technology. One flat structure could not manage all three. So Flipkart's leadership went through the five steps of organising.

  Step 1 — Identification and Division of Work: Flipkart's leadership identified the work needed across the group: e-commerce operations, fashion retail, digital payments, logistics, technology, finance, human resources, legal. Each was divided into sub-tasks — e-commerce was divided into seller management, catalogue, pricing, customer experience.

  Step 2 — Departmentalisation: The work was grouped into three verticals — Flipkart (e-commerce), Myntra (fashion), PhonePe (payments) — each as a separate department with its own CEO, plus shared corporate functions (finance, HR, legal) serving all three. This is departmentalisation by product.

  Step 3 — Assignment of Duties: Kalyan Krishnamurthy was assigned as Group CEO of Flipkart; the CEOs of Myntra and PhonePe were assigned their respective verticals. Each was given the authority to run their vertical and the responsibility to deliver its targets.

  Step 4 — Establishing Reporting Relationships: The Myntra and PhonePe CEOs reported to the Flipkart Group CEO; the Group CEO reported to Walmart's international division leadership. Clear reporting lines meant everyone knew who answered to whom.

  Step 5 — Coordination: Cross-vertical reviews were instituted so that, for example, PhonePe's payment infrastructure could be integrated into Flipkart's checkout, and Myntra's fashion catalogue could be cross-promoted on Flipkart's app.

  Now let us address the common error that costs ISC marks every year — the confusion between authority, responsibility, and accountability. These three are NOT interchangeable. Authority is the RIGHT to command — it flows downward (the Group CEO has the right to direct the Myntra CEO). Responsibility is the OBLIGATION to perform — it flows downward but must be accepted (the Myntra CEO is obligated to deliver Myntra's revenue target and accepts that obligation). Accountability is the ANSWERABILITY for results — it flows upward (the Myntra CEO is answerable to the Group CEO for whether the target was met). The direction of flow is the key: authority and responsibility flow downward, accountability flows upward. If you write "accountability flows downward" in the exam, you lose marks — accountability is always upward because the subordinate answers to the superior.

  This node builds on c12_comm_012 (planning) and c12_comm_013 (types of plans) because organising follows planning in the management process — the plan determines what activities are needed, and organising groups those activities into departments and assigns them to people. It also extends c11_comm_014 (joint stock company) — in Class 11 you learned the external legal form of a company (incorporation, shares, directors); now you learn how the internal structure of that company is organised into departments and reporting lines.

  The ethical dimension matters too. When Amazon India faced criticism in 2021 for warehouse workers having inadequate bathroom breaks, it revealed an organising failure — duty assignment had prioritised packing efficiency over worker welfare. Ethical organising requires that departmentalisation and duty assignment consider human limitations, not just output targets.

  The key takeaway: organising is a five-step process (division of work → departmentalisation → assignment of duties → reporting relationships → coordination) that turns a plan into a working structure. Master the authority-responsibility-accountability distinction, and you will answer any organising question correctly.
ELI5: |
  Imagine your school is hosting an annual day function. The principal first decides what needs to be done — stage, food, invitations, decorations (division of work). Then groups the work into committees — stage committee, food committee, decoration committee (departmentalisation). Then assigns each committee a teacher in charge (assignment of duties). Then decides that each committee teacher reports to the vice-principal (reporting relationships). Then holds a weekly meeting so all committees coordinate (coordination). That is organising — turning a plan into a working team.
COMMON_MISCONCEPTION: |
  Misconception 1: "Authority, responsibility, and accountability are the same thing — they all mean the manager's duty." Correction: They are distinct and flow in different directions. Authority is the RIGHT to command (flows downward from superior to subordinate). Responsibility is the OBLIGATION to perform the assigned task (flows downward but is accepted by the subordinate). Accountability is the ANSWERABILITY for results (flows upward — the subordinate answers to the superior). Example: The Jio CEO has the authority to allocate the marketing budget (right, downward); the Jio Sales head has the responsibility to achieve 50 million new subscribers (obligation, downward-accepted); the Jio Sales head is accountable to the Jio CEO for whether the target was met (answerability, upward). Writing that "accountability flows downward" is the most common exam error.

  Misconception 2: "Organising and planning are the same because both involve deciding what to do." Correction: Planning decides WHAT to achieve and HOW (broadly); organising decides WHO will do what work, in what groups, and reporting to whom. Planning produces objectives, strategies, policies; organising produces departments, job roles, and reporting lines. Reliance's plan for Jio (2016) was to launch a 4G telecom service; organising was creating the Jio division with Network, Sales, and Care departments and assigning each a head. The two functions are sequential and distinct.

  Misconception 3: "Departmentalisation means dividing work into small tasks." Correction: Division of work (Step 1) means breaking the total work into small tasks. Departmentalisation (Step 2) means GROUPING those small tasks into departments on the basis of function, product, territory, or customer. Division breaks down; departmentalisation groups together. Jio's network activity is first divided into tower installation, fibre laying, spectrum management (division of work), and then these are grouped together under the Jio Network department (departmentalisation). Confusing the two steps reverses the logic of the organising process.
DIAGNOSTIC_QUESTION: |
  Q1 (recall): Define organising. List the five steps of the organising process in order and explain each in one sentence with an Indian example. State four points of importance of organising.
  Answer: Organising is the process of identifying and grouping activities to be performed, assigning duties to individuals and departments, and establishing authority and reporting relationships among them so that organisational objectives can be achieved. The five steps are: (1) Identification and Division of Work — identifying the total work and breaking it into manageable activities; example: Reliance Jio identified network, sales, customer service as key activities and broke each into sub-tasks. (2) Departmentalisation — grouping the divided activities into departments; example: Jio's activities were grouped into Jio Network, Jio Sales, Jio Care departments. (3) Assignment of Duties — allocating tasks to specific individuals and granting them authority; example: each Jio department head was assigned specific targets and the authority to deploy resources. (4) Establishing Reporting Relationships — defining who reports to whom; example: the Jio Care head reports to the Jio CEO, who reports to the Reliance board. (5) Coordination of Activities — aligning the efforts of all departments toward objectives; example: Reliance's weekly cross-departmental reviews align network, sales, and care heads. Four points of importance: (a) benefits of specialisation (TCS engineers in BFSI vertical develop banking domain expertise); (b) clarity of roles (every employee knows their responsibility and reporting line); (c) effective administration (manageable span of control at each level); (d) growth and expansion (Jio scaled to 450 million subscribers as a separate division without disrupting Reliance's other businesses).

  Q2 (understand): Distinguish between authority, responsibility, and accountability. State the direction of flow for each. Then identify which of the three is being described in each of the following: (a) "The TCS project manager has the right to approve leave applications of team members." (b) "The TCS team lead is obligated to deliver the software module by 30 September 2025." (c) "The TCS team lead must explain to the project manager why the module delivery was delayed by two weeks."
  Answer: Authority is the right of a manager to command subordinates and deploy resources; it flows downward from superior to subordinate. Responsibility is the obligation of the subordinate to perform the assigned task; it flows downward but is accepted by the subordinate. Accountability is the answerability of the subordinate for the results of the assigned task; it flows upward because the subordinate answers to the superior. Classification: (a) Authority — the right to approve leave is a right to command, flowing downward from project manager to team members. (b) Responsibility — the obligation to deliver the module by a date is a duty assigned and accepted, flowing downward. (c) Accountability — the requirement to explain the delay is answerability for results, flowing upward from team lead to project manager.

  Q3 (apply): When Tata Motors acquired Jaguar Land Rover (JLR) from Ford in 2008 for $2.3 billion, it faced an organising decision: should JLR be merged into Tata Motors India, or kept as a separate subsidiary? Tata chose to keep JLR as a separate subsidiary with its own CEO (Ralf Speth, appointed 2010) and its own engineering, design, and sales departments in the UK. Apply the five steps of organising to this decision, showing how each step was executed.
  Answer: (1) Identification and Division of Work: Tata Motors identified the work needed to run JLR — luxury car design, engineering, manufacturing at UK plants (Solihull, Castle Bromwich, Halewood), global sales and marketing, after-sales service. Each was divided into sub-tasks — design was divided into exterior design, interior design, powertrain engineering. (2) Departmentalisation: Rather than merging these into Tata Motors India's existing departments, Tata grouped them into a separate JLR subsidiary organised by function (Design, Engineering, Manufacturing, Sales, Finance, HR) — a departmentalisation by product (the JLR luxury car product line). (3) Assignment of Duties: Ralf Speth was assigned as CEO of JLR with the authority to run the subsidiary and the responsibility to restore JLR to profitability; department heads were assigned their respective functions. (4) Establishing Reporting Relationships: The JLR CEO reports to the Tata Motors board (and ultimately to the Tata Sons board); JLR department heads report to the JLR CEO. JLR does NOT report through Tata Motors India's management, preserving its autonomy. (5) Coordination: Cross-company coordination occurs at the board level — Tata Motors and JLR share component platforms and engineering know-how where beneficial, but JLR's day-to-day operations are independent. This organising decision enabled JLR to turn around from a loss-making brand under Ford to contributing over ₹4,000 crore to Tata Motors' profits by 2014-15.

  Q4 (analyze/evaluate): "A well-organised company can grow indefinitely without re-organising, because the original structure will scale with the business." Evaluate this statement using the examples of ITC's diversification (from tobacco into FMCG, hotels, paper, agri-business) and Reliance Jio's launch. Is re-organisation ever necessary? If so, when and why?
  Answer: The statement is false. No organisational structure scales indefinitely without re-organisation, because growth in scale, product variety, or geography eventually strains the original structure beyond its design limits. ITC illustrates this clearly. ITC began as a single-product tobacco company with a functional structure (Production, Marketing, Finance, HR departments serving one tobacco business). When ITC diversified into FMCG (Aashirvaad, Sunfeast, Bingo), hotels (ITC Hotels), paperboards (ITC Paperboards), and agri-business (ITC Agri), the functional structure became unworkable — a single Marketing department could not effectively market cigarettes, biscuits, luxury hotels, and paperboards simultaneously, because each product has a different customer, competitor, and channel. ITC therefore re-organised into a divisional structure around 2001, with each division (FMCG, Hotels, Paperboards, Agri) having its own CEO, marketing, and finance teams. This re-organisation enabled ITC's FMCG revenues to grow to over ₹16,000 crore by 2023. Reliance Jio's launch in 2016 is another example: Reliance Industries had a structure oriented to its petrochemicals and refining businesses; launching a telecom business required creating a new Jio division with its own Network, Sales, and Care departments, because telecom has fundamentally different operations (spectrum, towers, SIM distribution, customer service) from petrochemicals. Re-organisation is necessary when (a) the company diversifies into products or markets that the original structure was not designed to serve, (b) the scale of operations exceeds the span of control that the original structure assumed, or (c) an acquisition or merger brings in operations that need either integration or autonomy. The correct evaluation: organising is not a one-time exercise but an ongoing function; structures must be re-organised whenever strategy changes, because structure follows strategy.

  Q5 (create): Construct a comparison table (in text form) comparing the five steps of organising on three parameters: (a) what the step does, (b) the key question it answers, and (c) one Indian example. Then design a one-sentence test that a student can use to verify whether a given managerial action belongs to the organising function (as opposed to planning, staffing, or directing).
  Answer: Comparison Table:
    Step | What it does | Key question | Indian example
    1. Identification & Division of Work | Breaks total work into manageable activities | What activities are needed? | Reliance Jio identified network, sales, customer service as key activities and divided each into sub-tasks.
    2. Departmentalisation | Groups activities into departments by function/product/territory/customer | How do we group these activities? | Jio's activities grouped into Jio Network, Jio Sales, Jio Care departments.
    3. Assignment of Duties | Allocates tasks to individuals and grants authority | Who does what, and with what authority? | Each Jio department head assigned specific targets and the authority to deploy resources.
    4. Establishing Reporting Relationships | Defines who reports to whom | Who answers to whom? | Jio Care head reports to Jio CEO; Jio CEO reports to Reliance board.
    5. Coordination of Activities | Aligns departmental efforts toward objectives | How do the parts work together? | Reliance's weekly cross-departmental reviews align network, sales, and care heads.

    One-sentence test: "Does this managerial action involve grouping activities into departments, assigning duties to people, or defining reporting relationships?" If yes → organising. If the action involves setting objectives or choosing strategies → planning. If it involves recruiting, selecting, or training people → staffing. If it involves motivating, supervising, or communicating → directing.

--- NODE c12_comm_015 ---
TITLE: Organisational Structure — Functional vs Divisional
DEFINITION: |
  Organisational structure is the framework that emerges from the organising process — specifically, from the departmentalisation step (Step 2 of organising, covered in node c12_comm_014). It defines how activities are grouped into departments, how authority flows, and how reporting relationships are arranged. The two principal types of organisational structure in the ISC syllabus are functional structure and divisional structure. The choice between them is one of the most consequential design decisions a company makes, because the wrong structure for a given business can stifle growth and create coordination failures.

  Functional structure groups activities by specialised functions — typically Marketing, Finance, Human Resources, Production, and Research & Development. Each department is headed by a functional specialist (a Marketing Manager, a Finance Manager) and serves the entire organisation. Tata Steel uses a functional structure: its Production department handles all steel-making across its Jamshedpur, Kalinganagar, and Durgapur plants; its Marketing department sells all steel products; its Finance department manages all financial operations; its HR department manages all 70,000+ employees. Asian Paints similarly uses a functional structure with departments for R&D, Manufacturing, Sales, Finance, and HR serving its single paint business. The features of functional structure are: (a) departments are created by function; (b) each department is staffed by specialists in that function; (c) all departments serve the entire organisation's single product line; (d) authority flows from the CEO down through functional heads to specialists.

  The merits of functional structure are three. First, functional specialisation: employees in the Marketing department develop deep marketing expertise because they handle marketing for the entire company, leading to professional excellence. Second, reduced duplication: there is one Marketing department, one Finance department — no duplication of functional resources across products. Third, easier control and coordination within each function: the Marketing Manager can coordinate all marketing activities centrally. The demerits are three. First, departmental silos: the Marketing department may lose sight of production realities, and Production may ignore customer needs surfaced by Marketing — cross-functional coordination becomes difficult. Second, slow decisions: when a decision spans multiple functions (e.g., launching a new steel grade requires Production, Marketing, and Finance agreement), it takes longer because each functional head must be consulted. Third, difficulty in fixing responsibility for product performance: since no single person is responsible for the product end-to-end (Production makes it, Marketing sells it, Finance prices it), accountability for overall product results is diffused. Functional structure is suitable for single-product or medium-sized companies — Tata Steel (steel only), Asian Paints (paint only).

  Divisional structure groups activities by product, territory, or customer. Each division is semi-autonomous and contains its own functional departments (its own Marketing, Finance, HR, Production). Hindustan Unilever Limited (HUL) uses a divisional structure organised by product categories: Home Care (Surf Excel, Vim, Domex), Beauty & Personal Care (Lux, Dove, Fair & Lovely), and Foods & Refreshment (Knorr, Kissan, Magnum). Each division has its own marketing, finance, and sales teams. Reliance Industries uses a divisional structure: Oil-to-Chemicals (O2C), Jio Digital Services, Reliance Retail, and New Energy — each a semi-autonomous division under the Reliance umbrella. ITC uses a divisional structure: FMCG (Aashirvaad, Sunfeast, Bingo), Hotels (ITC Hotels), Paperboards & Packaging, and Agri-Business. The features of divisional structure are: (a) departments are created by product, territory, or customer; (b) each division has its own functional departments; (c) each division operates semi-autonomously under a divisional head; (d) the corporate headquarters provides strategic oversight and shared services.

  The merits of divisional structure are three. First, divisional autonomy and faster decisions: each division can make product-specific decisions without consulting other divisions — HUL's Home Care division can launch a new Surf Excel variant without waiting for the Foods division to agree. Second, clear accountability for product performance: the head of HUL's Beauty & Personal Care division is accountable for the entire P&L of that division, end-to-end. Third, responsiveness to specific markets and customers: each division can tailor its products, pricing, and promotion to its specific customer base. The demerits are three. First, duplication of resources: each division has its own Marketing, Finance, and HR departments, so the same function is replicated across divisions — HUL has three marketing teams (one per division) instead of one. Second, higher cost: the duplication of functional resources increases administrative cost. Third, potential conflict between divisions: divisions may compete for corporate resources or cannibalise each other's customers, requiring strong corporate-level coordination. Divisional structure is suitable for large diversified companies — HUL, Reliance Industries, ITC.

  The legal framework reinforces the visibility of divisional structure. Schedule III of the Companies Act 2013 requires companies to report segment-wise financial information in their financial statements. Reliance Industries must report each division (O2C, Jio, Retail, New Energy) separately in its annual report, making the divisional structure visible in legally mandated disclosures. SEBI's Listing Regulations require listed companies to disclose segment-wise performance, enabling investors to evaluate whether a functional or divisional structure delivers better results for a given business.
TEXT_WALKTHROUGH: |
  In the previous node (c12_comm_014) you learned that departmentalisation is Step 2 of the organising process — the step where divided activities are grouped into departments. This node takes that step further: the BASIS on which you group activities determines whether you get a functional structure or a divisional structure. Group by function (Marketing, Finance, HR) and you get a functional structure. Group by product, territory, or customer and you get a divisional structure. The choice is not a matter of preference — it depends on whether the company is single-product or diversified.

  Let us walk through two contrasting Indian companies that sit at the top of India's corporate landscape but use opposite structures. Tata Steel uses a functional structure. Why? Because Tata Steel is essentially a single-product company — it makes steel. All its activities (mining iron ore, smelting, rolling, selling steel) serve one product. So it makes sense to group activities by function: one Production department handles all steel-making, one Marketing department sells all steel, one Finance department manages all money, one HR department manages all 70,000+ employees. There is no point creating a separate marketing team for "long products" and another for "flat products" when the customers (construction companies, automakers) often buy both.

  Now contrast this with Hindustan Unilever Limited (HUL). HUL sells Surf Excel (detergent), Lux (soap), Knorr (soup), Kissan (ketchup), Magnum (ice cream), Dove (shampoo), Domex (toilet cleaner) — products so different that one Marketing department cannot effectively market all of them. The customer for Surf Excel is a homemaker worried about stain removal; the customer for Magnum is a young adult seeking a premium dessert experience; the customer for Domex is a homemaker concerned about hygiene. One marketing team cannot serve all three. So HUL uses a divisional structure: Home Care division (with its own marketing, finance, sales), Beauty & Personal Care division (with its own marketing, finance, sales), Foods & Refreshment division (with its own marketing, finance, sales). Each division is semi-autonomous and accountable for its own P&L.

  Here is the common error that examiners catch every year. Students recommend a functional structure for a large diversified company like the Tata Group or ITC. This is wrong. Functional structure suits single-product or medium-sized companies (Tata Steel, Asian Paints). Divisional structure suits large diversified companies (HUL, Reliance, ITC). The deciding factor is product variety: if the company sells one main product, functional structure works; if the company sells many unrelated products, divisional structure is necessary. The error arises because students do not consider product variety as the deciding factor — they pick a structure based on company size or fame rather than on the structural logic.

  Consider ITC's history as a real-world illustration of structure following strategy. ITC began as a tobacco company with a functional structure — one Marketing department, one Production department, all serving the tobacco business. When ITC diversified into FMCG (Aashirvaad atta, Sunfeast biscuits), hotels (ITC Hotels), paperboards, and agri-business, the functional structure broke down — one Marketing department could not market cigarettes, biscuits, luxury hotels, and paperboards. So ITC re-organised into a divisional structure around 2001, with each division (FMCG, Hotels, Paperboards, Agri) having its own CEO, marketing, and finance teams. This re-organisation enabled ITC's FMCG revenues to grow to over ₹16,000 crore by 2023 — growth that the old functional structure could not have supported.

  The ethical dimension: divisional structure can create ethical silos. When Volkswagen's diesel division cheated on emissions tests in 2015 (the "Dieselgate" scandal), the divisional structure allowed the ethical breach to remain hidden from corporate oversight because each division operated semi-autonomously. Indian companies like the Tata Group mitigate this risk through the Tata Code of Conduct, which binds all divisions to the same ethical standards regardless of their autonomy.

  This node extends c11_comm_014 (joint stock company): in Class 11 you learned the external legal form of a company; now you learn the internal structural choice — functional or divisional — that the company must make. It also builds on c12_comm_014 (organising steps): departmentalisation (Step 2) produces the structure, and the basis of departmentalisation (function vs product) determines the structure type.

  The key takeaway: functional structure groups by function and suits single-product companies (Tata Steel, Asian Paints); divisional structure groups by product/territory/customer and suits diversified companies (HUL, Reliance, ITC). Structure follows strategy — when a company diversifies, it must shift from functional to divisional.
ELI5: |
  Think of a school. If the school teaches only one subject (say, only Maths), it makes sense to have departments by function — one Admissions department, one Exams department, one Results department — all serving Maths. That is functional structure. But if the school teaches Maths, Science, English, and Music, it makes sense to have a division for each subject — each with its own teacher, its own exam papers, its own results. That is divisional structure. One subject → functional. Many subjects → divisional.
COMMON_MISCONCEPTION: |
  Misconception 1: "A functional structure is best for large companies like Reliance because it is simpler to manage." Correction: Functional structure suits single-product or medium-sized companies, not large diversified companies. Reliance Industries operates in oil-to-chemicals, telecom (Jio), retail, and new energy — four fundamentally different businesses. A single Marketing department cannot market petrol, SIM cards, groceries, and solar panels. Reliance therefore uses a divisional structure, with each division having its own marketing, finance, and HR. The deciding factor is product variety, not company size. Recommending a functional structure for Reliance is the classic exam error.

  Misconception 2: "Divisional structure has no disadvantages because each division is autonomous and accountable." Correction: Divisional structure has three significant demerits. First, duplication of resources — each division has its own Marketing, Finance, and HR, so the same function is replicated across divisions (HUL has three marketing teams instead of one), increasing cost. Second, higher administrative cost due to this duplication. Third, potential conflict between divisions — divisions may compete for corporate resources or cannibalise each other's customers. Autonomy and accountability are merits, but they come with the cost of duplication and potential conflict.

  Misconception 3: "A company can use both functional and divisional structure at the same time at the same level." Correction: At a given level of the organisation, the structure is either functional or divisional, not both. A company may use functional structure at the division level (each HUL division has functional departments like Marketing, Finance, HR within it) — this is a divisional structure with functional departments inside each division, not a hybrid at the same level. The top-level choice is one or the other. The matrix structure (which combines both) is outside the ISC syllabus for this node.
DIAGNOSTIC_QUESTION: |
  Q1 (recall): Define functional structure and divisional structure. List three merits and three demerits of each. State which type of company each structure is suitable for, with one Indian example each.
  Answer: Functional structure groups activities by specialised functions (Marketing, Finance, HR, Production, R&D), with each department serving the entire organisation. Merits: (1) functional specialisation — employees develop deep expertise in their function; (2) reduced duplication — one Marketing department, one Finance department, no replication; (3) easier control and coordination within each function. Demerits: (1) departmental silos — cross-functional coordination is difficult; (2) slow decisions when issues span multiple functions; (3) diffused accountability for product performance — no single person is responsible end-to-end. Suitable for single-product or medium-sized companies. Example: Tata Steel (steel only), Asian Paints (paint only). Divisional structure groups activities by product, territory, or customer, with each division being semi-autonomous and containing its own functional departments. Merits: (1) divisional autonomy and faster decisions — each division can act independently; (2) clear accountability for product performance — the division head owns the entire P&L; (3) responsiveness to specific markets and customers. Demerits: (1) duplication of resources — each division has its own Marketing, Finance, HR; (2) higher administrative cost due to duplication; (3) potential conflict between divisions over resources or customers. Suitable for large diversified companies. Example: HUL (Home Care, Beauty & Personal Care, Foods & Refreshment divisions), Reliance Industries (O2C, Jio, Retail, New Energy divisions), ITC (FMCG, Hotels, Paperboards, Agri divisions).

  Q2 (understand): Distinguish between functional structure and divisional structure on five parameters in a tabular format: (a) basis of departmentalisation, (b) suitability, (c) coordination, (d) cost, (e) specialisation. Then classify each of the following three Indian companies as functional or divisional with justification: (i) Amul (Gujarat Cooperative Milk Marketing Federation), (ii) Godrej Group, (iii) Maruti Suzuki India Limited.
  Answer: Comparison Table:
    Parameter | Functional Structure | Divisional Structure
    Basis of departmentalisation | By function (Marketing, Finance, HR, Production) | By product, territory, or customer
    Suitability | Single-product or medium-sized companies | Large diversified companies
    Coordination | Strong within function, weak across functions (silos) | Strong within division, requires corporate-level coordination across divisions
    Cost | Lower (no duplication of functions) | Higher (duplication of functions in each division)
    Specialisation | Functional specialisation (deep expertise in one function) | Product/territory specialisation (broad expertise across functions for one product/territory)
    Classification: (i) Amul — Functional structure. Amul is essentially a single-product company (dairy and dairy products — milk, butter, cheese, ice cream, ghee). All activities (procurement from farmers, processing, marketing, distribution) serve one product line, so a functional structure with Procurement, Processing, Marketing, and Finance departments is appropriate. (ii) Godrej Group — Divisional structure. Godrej operates in diverse businesses: Godrej Consumer Products (soaps, hair colour), Godrej Agrovet (agri-inputs), Godrej Properties (real estate), Godrej Industries (chemicals), Godrej Aerospace. These are fundamentally different products with different customers, so each operates as a separate division/company under the Godrej umbrella. (iii) Maruti Suzuki India Limited — Functional structure. Maruti Suzuki is essentially a single-product company (passenger vehicles — Swift, Baleno, Brezza, Dzire). All activities (manufacturing at Manesar and Gurgaon, R&D, marketing, finance, HR) serve one automobile product line, so a functional structure is appropriate.

  Q3 (apply): A hypothetical Indian company, "Bharat Consumer Goods Ltd" (BCGL), started in 1995 making only soap (brand: "Saatvik"). By 2025, BCGL has diversified into: (a) soaps and detergents (Saatvik), (b) packaged foods (FreshKart atta, rice, spices), (c) ayurvedic medicines (AyurLife), and (d) home appliances (CoolHome fans, mixer-grinders). BCGL currently uses a functional structure with one Marketing department, one Finance department, one HR department, and one Production department serving all four product lines. BCGL's revenue has stagnated at ₹2,000 crore for three years, and internal reviews show that the Marketing department cannot effectively market both ayurvedic medicines (which need doctor endorsements and pharmacy channels) and home appliances (which need electronics retail channels). Recommend whether BCGL should change its organisational structure. If yes, which structure and why? If no, why not?
  Answer: BCGL should change from a functional structure to a divisional structure. The reasoning: BCGL has diversified from a single product (soap) in 1995 to four fundamentally different product lines by 2025 — soaps/detergents (FMCG hygiene), packaged foods (FMCG food), ayurvedic medicines (healthcare, regulated by the Drugs and Cosmetics Act 1940), and home appliances (consumer durables, electronics retail). These four product lines have different customers (a homemaker for soap, a cook for atta, a patient for ayurvedic medicine, a household for a fan), different distribution channels (grocery stores for soap and atta, pharmacies for ayurvedic medicine, electronics stores for appliances), different regulatory requirements, and different marketing approaches. A single Marketing department cannot effectively serve all four — the stagnation at ₹2,000 crore and the internal review finding confirm this. BCGL should re-organise into four divisions: Saatvik (soaps and detergents), FreshKart (packaged foods), AyurLife (ayurvedic medicines), and CoolHome (home appliances). Each division should have its own Marketing, Finance, HR, and Production departments, with a divisional CEO accountable for the P&L of that division. This will enable faster product-specific decisions, clear accountability for each product line's performance, and responsiveness to each market. The cost of duplication (four marketing teams instead of one) is justified by the revenue growth that specialisation will unlock — ITC's similar shift to a divisional structure in 2001 enabled its FMCG revenues to grow to over ₹16,000 crore by 2023. The functional structure was appropriate when BCGL made only soap; it is no longer appropriate now that BCGL is a diversified four-product company. Structure must follow strategy.

  Q4 (analyze/evaluate): "Divisional structure is always superior to functional structure because it provides autonomy, accountability, and responsiveness." Evaluate this statement. Under what circumstances would a functional structure outperform a divisional structure? Use the examples of Tata Steel and HUL to support your analysis.
  Answer: The statement is false. Divisional structure is not always superior; it is superior only for large diversified companies. For single-product or medium-sized companies, functional structure outperforms divisional structure for three reasons. First, functional structure avoids duplication of resources — Tata Steel has one Marketing department, one Finance department, one HR department serving its entire steel business. If Tata Steel adopted a divisional structure (e.g., a "Long Products" division and a "Flat Products" division, each with its own marketing and finance teams), it would duplicate functional resources for no benefit, because the customers (construction companies, automakers) often buy both long and flat products and are best served by one marketing team that understands their total steel needs. Second, functional structure is lower cost — for a single-product company, the duplication cost of divisional structure (multiple marketing, finance, HR teams) is pure overhead with no offsetting benefit. Third, functional structure provides deeper functional specialisation — Tata Steel's Marketing department handles all steel marketing, so its marketers develop deep steel-marketing expertise, whereas a divisional marketer for "Long Products" would have a narrower scope. HUL, by contrast, benefits from divisional structure because its three product categories (Home Care, Beauty & Personal Care, Foods & Refreshment) are so different that one Marketing department cannot serve all three effectively — the customer for Surf Excel (stain-removal-focused homemaker) is different from the customer for Magnum (premium dessert-seeking young adult). For HUL, the duplication cost of three marketing teams is justified by the autonomy, accountability, and market responsiveness that each division gains. The correct evaluation: neither structure is universally superior. The choice depends on product variety. Single-product companies (Tata Steel, Asian Paints, Maruti Suzuki) benefit from functional structure (no duplication, deep specialisation, lower cost). Diversified companies (HUL, Reliance, ITC) benefit from divisional structure (autonomy, accountability, responsiveness, despite higher cost). Recommending divisional structure for a single-product company would waste resources on unnecessary duplication; recommending functional structure for a diversified company would create coordination failures and stifle growth. The deciding factor is the degree of product diversification, not the inherent superiority of one structure.

  Q5 (create): Design a decision framework (in text form) that an ISC student can use to recommend either a functional or divisional structure for any given Indian company. The framework must use at least three decision questions, each answered yes/no, leading to a recommendation. Then test your framework on two companies not used in the DEFINITION or TEXT_WALKTHROUGH: (a) Dabur India (ayurvedic and natural healthcare products, FMCG, foods), and (b) Bajaj Auto (motorcycles and three-wheelers).
  Answer: Decision Framework:
    Step 1: Does the company operate in more than one fundamentally different product line or industry (i.e., products with different customers, channels, or regulatory environments)?
      → If NO → Functional structure is recommended. Stop.
      → If YES → Go to Step 2.
    Step 2: Are the product lines large and complex enough that each would benefit from its own dedicated functional teams (marketing, finance, HR)?
      → If NO → Functional structure may still suffice (the product lines are too small to justify duplication). Stop.
      → If YES → Go to Step 3.
    Step 3: Does the company need clear P&L accountability for each product line separately (e.g., for investor reporting under Schedule III of the Companies Act 2013, or for internal performance management)?
      → If YES → Divisional structure is recommended.
      → If NO → Divisional structure is still likely beneficial, but functional with product-level teams may work.

    Test (a): Dabur India.
      Step 1: Does Dabur operate in more than one fundamentally different product line? YES — Dabur has healthcare (ayurvedic medicines like Dabur Chyawanprash, Honitus), personal care (Dabur Vatika hair oil, Dabur Gulabari rose water), and foods (Dabur Hommade pastes, Réal fruit juice). These have different customers (patients vs personal-care consumers vs beverage consumers) and different channels (pharmacies for medicines, grocery for personal care and foods). Step 2: Are the product lines large enough for dedicated functional teams? YES — Dabur's revenue exceeds ₹12,000 crore (FY 2023-24), and each category is substantial. Step 3: Does Dabur need separate P&L accountability? YES — Dabur reports segment-wise results. → Recommendation: Divisional structure. ✓ (Dabur in fact uses a divisional structure with Healthcare, Personal Care, and Foods divisions.)

    Test (b): Bajaj Auto.
      Step 1: Does Bajaj Auto operate in more than one fundamentally different product line? Bajaj Auto makes motorcycles (Pulsar, Dominar, Platina) and three-wheelers (RE, Maxima). These are both automotive products sold through similar dealer networks to similar customer segments (commuters, commercial users). The product lines are related, not fundamentally different. → NO → Functional structure is recommended. ✓ (Bajaj Auto, being essentially a single-product automobile company, uses a functional structure with R&D, Manufacturing, Marketing, Finance, and HR departments serving its two-wheeler and three-wheeler business.)

  Q2 fails -> Review the DEFINITION and the COMPARATIVE_FRAMEWORK field, then re-read the TEXT_WALKTHROUGH's Tata Steel vs HUL contrast. The gap is constructing a comparison table and classifying companies based on product variety. After review, retry Q2.

  Q3 fails -> Review the TEXT_WALKTHROUGH's ITC re-organisation example (functional to divisional upon diversification) and the COMMON_MISCONCEPTION on product variety as the deciding factor, then re-read the DEFINITION paragraphs on suitability.   Q4 fails -> Review the DEFINITION paragraphs on merits and demerits of both structures and the TEXT_WALKTHROUGH's Tata Steel vs HUL contrast, then re-read the COMMON_MISCONCEPTION on "divisional is always superior". MASTERY_CRITERIA: |
  Can define functional structure (grouping by specialised functions like marketing, finance, HR) and divisional structure (grouping by product, territory, or customer) with one Indian example each; can list three merits and three demerits of each structure; can construct a comparison table on five parameters (basis, suitability, coordination, cost, specialisation); can recommend which structure suits a given Indian company scenario with justification based on product variety as the deciding factor; can explain that structure follows strategy and that a company must shift from functional to divisional when it diversifies.
SCOPE_BOUNDARY: |
  Teach: meaning, features, three merits, and three demerits of functional and divisional structures; a comparison table on five parameters; the suitability criterion (single-product vs diversified); the legal requirement of segment-wise reporting under Schedule III of the Companies Act 2013 and SEBI Listing Regulations; the recommendation logic (product variety as the deciding factor).
  Do NOT teach: matrix structure (not in the ISC syllabus for this node); line and staff organisation; project organisation; span of control; centralisation and decentralisation (later nodes); formal vs informal organisation (node c12_comm_016).
