--- NODE c12_comm_024 ---
TITLE: Coordination — Meaning; Coordination as Essence of Management
DEFINITION: |
  Coordination is the synchronisation of the activities, efforts, and actions of different departments, units, and individuals within an organisation to ensure that they work harmoniously and effectively toward the achievement of common organisational goals. It is the process of integrating the disparate and often competing objectives of specialised departments — production, marketing, finance, human resources, procurement — into a unified, coherent effort. Without coordination, each department would optimise its own sub-goal at the expense of the organisation's overall goal: the production department would maximise output (creating inventory that finance must fund and marketing must sell), the finance department would minimise cost (starving production of raw materials and marketing of advertising budget), and the marketing department would maximise promotion (generating orders that production cannot fulfil). Coordination is the managerial function that prevents this sub-optimisation by aligning each department's efforts with the organisation's collective objective.

  Coordination is called the "essence of management" — not a separate sixth function alongside planning, organising, staffing, directing, and controlling, but the integrating thread that runs through all five functions and makes them work as a coherent whole. The metaphor is that of a thread running through a necklace of beads: the beads (the five functions) are distinct, but without the thread (coordination), they scatter and the necklace has no form. Specifically, coordination permeates each of the five functions: (1) Planning coordinates objectives — when Reliance Jio launched in September 2016, the plan had to coordinate the objectives of the network team (pan-India 4G coverage), the marketing team (free-voice and cheap-data positioning), and the sales team (SIM distribution through 1.5 lakh retail outlets); without this coordination, the launch would have failed despite each department's individual excellence. (2) Organising coordinates authority and responsibility — at Tata Steel, the departments (blast furnace, rolling mill, despatch, finance, HR) are linked through the scalar chain so that decisions flow up and instructions flow down without gaps or overlaps; organising creates the structural framework within which coordination operates. (3) Staffing coordinates human resources with job requirements — Infosys coordinates its hiring plans with its project pipeline, recruiting 1,000 engineers when the project order book shows $100 million of new work, so that people are available when projects start and not idle when projects end. (4) Directing coordinates individual effort with group goals — a Maruti Suzuki shift supervisor coordinates 40 assembly-line workers so that each worker's task (fitting doors, installing seats, testing lights) is performed in the right sequence and at the right time, producing a complete car every 90 seconds. (5) Controlling coordinates actual performance with planned standards — Tata Motors coordinates its monthly production with its sales plan, so that the number of cars produced matches the number dealers can sell, avoiding both stockouts (lost sales) and excess inventory (capital tied up). In each function, coordination is not an add-on but the integrating principle that makes the function effective.

  The importance of coordination is illustrated most powerfully by contrasting Indian examples of coordination success and coordination failure. Coordination success: Amul (the Gujarat Cooperative Milk Marketing Federation, GCMMF), established in 1946 under the leadership of Verghese Kurien, is India's most celebrated example of coordination. Three point six million milk producers across 18,000+ village dairy societies in Gujarat coordinate daily through a three-tier structure — village society (collects milk from farmers) → district union (processes milk into products) → state federation (markets Amul-branded products nationwide) — to collect, process, and distribute over 300 lakh litres of milk per day. This coordination, sustained for nearly 80 years, has made Amul a brand with annual turnover exceeding ₹55,000 crore (2023), transforming India from a milk-deficient nation in 1946 to the world's largest milk producer. Operation Flood (1970-1996), the world's largest dairy development programme, was built on this coordination model. Coordination failure: Kingfisher Airlines, founded by Vijay Mallya in 2005, collapsed in 2012 with accumulated debt exceeding ₹9,000 crore. The collapse was fundamentally a failure of coordination: the marketing department launched new international routes and a premium service (Kingfisher First) to compete with Jet Airways, while the finance department could not fund the expansion (aviation fuel costs had risen, and the 2008 financial crisis had tightened credit), and the operations department could not maintain the aircraft (lease payments were overdue, and lessors repossessed planes). Each department pursued its own objective — marketing pursued growth, finance pursued cost control, operations pursued fleet maintenance — but no one coordinated these objectives with the company's overall financial capacity. The result: employees went unpaid for months, vendors were not settled, passengers were stranded as flights were cancelled, and the airline's operating licence was suspended by the Directorate General of Civil Aviation (DGCA) in October 2012. The Kingfisher collapse is a textbook case of coordination failure — each department was competent in isolation, but the organisation failed because the departments' efforts were not synchronised.

  The legal and institutional dimension of coordination in India is significant. Section 149(4) of the Companies Act 2013 read with Rule 3 of the Companies (Meetings of Board and its Powers) Rules 2014 requires every listed company to have certain mandatory committees of the Board — the Audit Committee, the Nomination and Remuneration Committee, the Stakeholders Relationship Committee, and (for companies meeting specified thresholds) the Corporate Social Responsibility Committee. These committees are coordination mechanisms: the Audit Committee coordinates the board's oversight of financial reporting and internal controls, the CSR Committee coordinates the company's social responsibility activities, and the Nomination and Remuneration Committee coordinates the board's composition and executive pay. The Act legally mandates coordination between the board's various oversight functions, recognising that without structural coordination mechanisms, the board's oversight would be fragmented and ineffective. At the national level, NITI Aayog (established January 1, 2015, replacing the Planning Commission) serves as India's apex coordination body between the Central government and the State governments. Its Governing Council, chaired by the Prime Minister and including all Chief Ministers, coordinates national development priorities across state boundaries — mirroring at the governmental level how coordination synchronises organisational functions at the corporate level. The most celebrated recent Indian example of coordination is ISRO's Chandrayaan-3 Moon landing on August 23, 2023 — the launch vehicle team at Sriharikota, the satellite team at Bengaluru, the mission control team, and the ground station teams across the world coordinated with millisecond precision to achieve the first-ever soft landing on the Moon's south pole. ISRO Chairman S. Somanath called it "the result of perfect coordination across 15 ISRO centres" — a triumph that no amount of individual departmental excellence could have achieved without coordination.

  The ethical dimension of coordination arises when departments have conflicting goals that affect stakeholders. At Kingfisher Airlines, the marketing department's duty to customers (launching new routes, providing premium service) conflicted with the finance department's duty to shareholders (funding sustainability) and to employees (paying salaries). When coordination failed, all stakeholders suffered — employees lost jobs, vendors were not paid, passengers were stranded, and shareholders lost their investment. Ethical coordination requires that no single department's goals override the organisation's collective responsibility to all stakeholders; the manager's coordinating role is, in part, an ethical role of balancing competing legitimate claims.
TEXT_WALKTHROUGH: |
  You have now studied all five functions of management — planning (node c12_comm_012), organising (node c12_comm_014), staffing (node c12_comm_017), directing (node c12_comm_021), and controlling (node c12_comm_023). This final node of the management spiral asks: what makes these five functions work together as a coherent whole, rather than as five separate activities pulling in different directions? The answer is coordination — and the syllabus calls it the "essence of management," not a sixth function. This distinction is the single most important point in this node, and the most common source of lost marks in the board examination.

  Begin with a real Indian scenario that every student has witnessed. You buy a packet of Amul butter from your local Kirana store. Behind that ₹58 packet lies one of the world's most extraordinary coordination achievements. At 4 a.m. in a village in Banaskantha district, Gujarat, a farmer brings two litres of milk from his buffalo to the village dairy cooperative society. The society tests the milk for fat content, records the quantity against the farmer's account, and sends it by tanker to the district union's dairy plant in Palanpur. The district union pasteurises the milk, separates the cream, churns it into butter, packs it into 100-gram and 500-gram packs bearing the Amul girl logo, and dispatches it to the state federation's warehouse. The state federation (GCMMF) distributes the butter to 18,000+ dealers across India, from whom it reaches your Kirana store. Three point six million farmers, 18,000+ village societies, 18 district unions, and one state federation coordinate this flow every single day — and the farmer is paid for his milk within 24 hours through direct bank transfer. This is coordination: the synchronisation of millions of separate actions into a single, coherent flow that delivers butter to your breakfast table and income to the farmer. Without coordination, the farmer's milk would spoil in the village, the dairy plant would sit idle, and the Kirana store would have no butter to sell — despite each participant being individually capable.

  Now ask yourself: is coordination a separate sixth function of management, alongside planning, organising, staffing, directing, and controlling? The syllabus says no — and this is the common error. Students list coordination as "function 6" and lose marks. Coordination is not a separate function; it is the essence that permeates all five functions. Think of it this way: planning without coordination produces plans that conflict across departments; organising without coordination produces structures with gaps and overlaps; staffing without coordination produces people without matching jobs; directing without coordination produces effort without alignment; controlling without coordination produces measurement without corrective integration. Coordination is the thread that runs through each function and makes it effective — it is not a sixth bead on the necklace but the thread that holds the five beads together.

  To see coordination permeating each function, take the example of Reliance Jio's launch in September 2016. The planning function coordinated the objectives of three departments: network (pan-India 4G coverage by March 2017), marketing (free voice and data at ₹50 per GB), and sales (SIM distribution through 1.5 lakh retail outlets). The organising function coordinated the authority and responsibility of the network team, the marketing team, and the sales team under a unified launch command. The staffing function coordinated the recruitment of 5,000 field engineers with the network rollout schedule. The directing function coordinated the efforts of the field engineers, the marketing executives, and the retail distributors toward the September 5 launch date. The controlling function coordinated the actual subscriber additions (16 million in the first month — a world record) with the network capacity plan, triggering capacity expansion where demand exceeded plan. Every function was coordinated; without that coordination, Jio would not have acquired 100 million subscribers in 170 days — the fastest in the world.

  Now contrast this with Kingfisher Airlines' collapse in 2012. The marketing department launched new international routes and a premium class. The finance department could not fund them. The operations department could not maintain the aircraft because lease payments were overdue. Each department was competent, but their efforts were not coordinated with the company's overall financial capacity. The airline collapsed with ₹9,000+ crore in debt, employees unpaid, passengers stranded. This is coordination failure — and it shows that individual departmental excellence is meaningless without coordination.

  The ethical dimension: at Kingfisher, the marketing department's goal (growth, customer service) conflicted with the finance department's goal (cost control, shareholder returns) and the employees' interest (salary payment). When coordination failed, all stakeholders suffered. Ethical coordination requires that no single department's goals override the organisation's collective responsibility to all stakeholders. The manager's coordinating role is therefore partly an ethical role — balancing competing legitimate claims.

  Key takeaway: coordination is the synchronisation of efforts across departments and individuals toward common goals. It is not a sixth function but the essence that permeates all five functions of management. Amul is India's greatest coordination success; Kingfisher is India's most studied coordination failure. The Companies Act 2013 mandates coordination through board committees; NITI Aayog coordinates Centre and States; ISRO's Chandrayaan-3 was a triumph of coordination across 15 centres. Master this node and you have completed the management spiral from planning through coordination.
ELI5: |
  Imagine your family is preparing a festival dinner. Your father buys vegetables, your mother cooks, your sister sets the table, and you serve the food. If everyone does their part at the right time and in the right order, the dinner is ready together and everyone eats happily — that is coordination. But if your father buys vegetables late, your mother starts cooking before the vegetables arrive, and your sister sets the table after the food is cold, the dinner is a mess — that is coordination failure. Coordination is making sure all the parts fit together at the right time to achieve the common goal.
COMMON_MISCONCEPTION: |
  Misconception 1: "Coordination is a separate sixth function of management, alongside planning, organising, staffing, directing, and controlling." Correction: Coordination is NOT a separate function. The ISC syllabus explicitly calls it the "essence of management" — the integrating thread that permeates all five functions, not a sixth function. Listing it as "function 6" in an exam answer loses marks because it misrepresents the syllabus. Coordination is to the five functions what a thread is to a necklace of beads: the beads are the functions, the thread is coordination — without the thread, the beads scatter, but the thread is not itself a sixth bead.

  Misconception 2: "Coordination and cooperation are the same thing." Correction: Cooperation is the voluntary willingness of individuals to help each other — an attitude. Coordination is the managerial process of synchronising efforts toward a common goal — an organisational mechanism. A team may cooperate (everyone is friendly and willing to help) but still lack coordination (efforts are not synchronised, tasks overlap or leave gaps). At Kingfisher Airlines, employees may have cooperated personally but the departments were not coordinated — marketing launched routes that finance could not fund. Cooperation is necessary but not sufficient; coordination is the deliberate managerial action that aligns efforts.

  Misconception 3: "Coordination is needed only in large organisations like Amul or Tata; small businesses do not need it." Correction: Coordination is needed in every organisation, regardless of size, because any organisation with more than one person has the potential for unaligned effort. A two-person Kirana shop — the owner buys stock, the helper sells it — needs coordination: the owner must buy what the helper can sell, and the helper must sell what the owner has bought. Without coordination, the shop has stock that does not sell (capital tied up) or customers for products that are out of stock (lost sales). The scale of coordination differs (Amul coordinates 3.6 million farmers; the Kirana shop coordinates two people), but the principle is identical.
DIAGNOSTIC_QUESTION: |
  Q1 (recall): Define coordination. Explain why coordination is called the "essence of management" rather than a separate sixth function. Link coordination to each of the five functions of management with one Indian example per function.
  Answer: Coordination is the synchronisation of the activities, efforts, and actions of different departments, units, and individuals within an organisation to ensure that they work harmoniously toward the achievement of common organisational goals. It is called the "essence of management" rather than a separate sixth function because it is not a distinct function alongside planning, organising, staffing, directing, and controlling — it is the integrating thread that permeates all five functions and makes them work as a coherent whole. The metaphor is a thread through a necklace of beads: the beads are the five functions, the thread is coordination. Linking coordination to each function: (1) Planning — Reliance Jio's 2016 launch plan coordinated the objectives of the network, marketing, and sales teams. (2) Organising — Tata Steel's departments are linked through the scalar chain so authority and responsibility flow without gaps. (3) Staffing — Infosys coordinates its hiring plans with its project pipeline, recruiting engineers when new projects are won. (4) Directing — a Maruti Suzuki shift supervisor coordinates 40 assembly-line workers so each task is performed in the right sequence, producing a car every 90 seconds. (5) Controlling — Tata Motors coordinates monthly production with the sales plan, avoiding both stockouts and excess inventory.

  Q2 (understand): Distinguish between coordination and cooperation. A student says, "If all employees cooperate and are friendly with each other, the organisation is coordinated." Explain why this statement is incorrect, using the Kingfisher Airlines collapse as an illustration.
  Answer: The statement is incorrect because cooperation and coordination are different concepts. Cooperation is the voluntary willingness of individuals to help each other — it is an attitude and a personal disposition. Coordination is the managerial process of synchronising efforts toward a common goal — it is an organisational mechanism that deliberately aligns tasks, schedules, and objectives across departments. A team may cooperate (everyone is friendly and willing to help) but still lack coordination (efforts are not synchronised, tasks overlap or leave gaps, departments pursue conflicting sub-goals). The Kingfisher Airlines collapse of 2012 illustrates this distinction. Kingfisher's employees may well have cooperated personally — pilots, cabin crew, engineers, and marketing executives could have been individually helpful and friendly. But the departments were not coordinated: the marketing department launched new international routes and a premium class (Kingfisher First) to compete with Jet Airways, while the finance department could not fund the expansion (aviation fuel costs had risen and the 2008 financial crisis had tightened credit), and the operations department could not maintain the aircraft because lease payments were overdue and lessors repossessed planes. Each department's efforts were not synchronised with the company's overall financial capacity. The result was coordination failure: employees went unpaid for months, vendors were not settled, passengers were stranded as flights were cancelled, and the DGCA suspended the airline's operating licence in October 2012 with accumulated debt exceeding ₹9,000 crore. Cooperation (personal willingness to help) could not prevent the collapse because what was missing was coordination (the managerial alignment of departmental objectives with organisational capacity). The statement therefore confuses an attitude (cooperation) with a mechanism (coordination) and is incorrect.

  Q3 (apply): A new CEO takes charge of a mid-sized Indian e-commerce company based in Gurugram. The company has three departments — technology (builds the app and website), operations (manages warehouses and delivery), and marketing (runs ads and promotions) — that have been working in silos. Marketing runs a "Big Sale" promotion without informing operations, generating 5 lakh orders that the warehouses cannot pack in time, leading to 2 lakh customer cancellations and a flood of negative reviews. The technology team, meanwhile, releases an app update on the same day that crashes under the sale's traffic. Identify the coordination failures, recommend three specific coordination mechanisms the CEO should implement, and explain how each mechanism would have prevented the Big Sale disaster.
  Answer: The coordination failures are: (1) Marketing did not coordinate with operations before launching the Big Sale — operations was not informed of the expected order volume and could not scale warehouse staffing or packing capacity, leading to 2 lakh cancellations. (2) Marketing did not coordinate with technology — the technology team was not informed of the expected traffic surge and released an app update on the same day that crashed under the load, compounding the disaster. (3) There was no cross-departmental planning process — each department worked in a silo, optimising its own sub-goal (marketing maximised promotion, technology released its update on schedule, operations maintained its normal staffing) without aligning with the organisation's goal of a successful sale. Recommended coordination mechanisms: (a) A weekly cross-functional coordination meeting chaired by the CEO, attended by the heads of technology, operations, and marketing, where upcoming promotions, app releases, and warehouse capacity are discussed and aligned. This would have prevented the Big Sale disaster because marketing would have presented the sale plan at the meeting, operations would have flagged the capacity gap, and the sale would have been scaled to match warehouse capacity or the sale date would have been moved to allow operations to hire temporary staff. (b) A mandatory pre-launch checklist requiring any department launching a major initiative (a sale, an app update, a new product line) to obtain sign-off from the other two affected departments at least 14 days in advance. This would have prevented the disaster because marketing would have needed operations' sign-off (operations would have said "we cannot pack 5 lakh orders in 3 days, reduce the scale or give us 2 weeks to scale up") and technology's sign-off (technology would have said "do not launch the sale on the same day as our app update, move one of them"). (c) A shared dashboard (a real-time information system) showing each department's key metrics — marketing's expected order volume from the sale, operations' current packing capacity, technology's app stability status — so that any department can see the others' constraints in real time. This would have prevented the disaster because marketing would have seen on the dashboard that operations' packing capacity was 1 lakh orders per day and technology's app was undergoing an update, and would have scaled the sale accordingly. Each mechanism addresses a different dimension of coordination: the meeting addresses planning coordination, the checklist addresses process coordination, and the dashboard addresses information coordination.

  Q4 (evaluate): "Amul's success and Kingfisher's failure are two sides of the same coin — coordination. The difference is not in the competence of individual participants but in the presence or absence of a coordinating mechanism that aligns their efforts." Evaluate this statement with reference to Amul's three-tier structure, Kingfisher's departmental silos, and the role of NITI Aayog as a national coordination body.
  Answer: The statement is strongly supported by the evidence from both organisations and by the institutional parallel of NITI Aayog. Amul's success is built on a three-tier coordination structure that has operated for nearly 80 years: the village dairy cooperative society (tier 1) collects milk from farmers and tests it for fat content; the district milk union (tier 2) processes the milk into products (butter, ghee, cheese, milk powder) at its dairy plant; the state federation, GCMMF (tier 3) markets the Amul-branded products nationwide and remits payment back down the tiers. Each tier has a defined role, and the tiers are linked by formal mechanisms — milk collection times, tanker schedules, payment cycles (farmers are paid within 24 hours through direct bank transfer), and a shared brand (Amul). The coordination is structural and institutional, not dependent on the personal qualities of any individual farmer or manager. Three point six million farmers, 18,000+ village societies, 18 district unions, and one state federation coordinate daily to handle over 300 lakh litres of milk, generating annual turnover exceeding ₹55,000 crore (2023). The competence of each participant (a farmer with two buffaloes, a dairy plant technician, a marketing executive) is unremarkable in isolation — what is remarkable is the coordination that aligns millions of such participants into a coherent flow. Kingfisher Airlines' failure is the mirror image. Kingfisher had competent departments — a marketing team that won awards for its campaigns, an operations team that maintained a fleet of A320s and ATRs, a finance team that managed a complex capital structure. But the departments operated in silos: marketing launched new routes and a premium class (Kingfisher First) to compete with Jet Airways, without coordinating with finance on funding capacity; finance could not fund the expansion because aviation fuel costs had risen and the 2008 financial crisis had tightened credit; operations could not maintain the aircraft because lease payments were overdue and lessors repossessed planes. There was no structural coordination mechanism — no cross-functional committee, no integrated business plan, no shared dashboard — that aligned the departments' objectives with the company's overall financial capacity. The competence of each department was rendered useless by the absence of coordination. The airline collapsed in 2012 with accumulated debt exceeding ₹9,000 crore. The institutional parallel is NITI Aayog, established January 1, 2015, replacing the Planning Commission. NITI Aayog's Governing Council, chaired by the Prime Minister and including all Chief Ministers, is a coordination mechanism that aligns the development priorities of the Central government with those of the State governments — mirroring at the national level what Amul's three-tier structure does at the dairy level and what Kingfisher lacked at the corporate level. NITI Aayog exists precisely because the Centre and the States, like the departments of a company, can pursue conflicting sub-goals (a State may resist a Central infrastructure project for political reasons) unless a structural coordination mechanism aligns them. The statement is therefore correct: competence is necessary but not sufficient; coordination is the differentiating factor that determines whether competent parts produce a competent whole (Amul) or a failed whole (Kingfisher). The presence or absence of a coordinating mechanism — not the competence of individual participants — is the coin that separates success from failure.

  Q5 (create): Construct a comparison table for coordination success (Amul) and coordination failure (Kingfisher Airlines) with the following six columns: (a) Organisation, (b) Scale, (c) Departments/participants coordinated, (d) Coordination mechanism, (e) Outcome, (f) Lesson for managers. Then, below the table, design a simple five-point coordination checklist that any Indian business could use to test whether its departments are coordinated, with one yes/no question for each of the five management functions.
  Answer: Comparison Table:
  | Dimension | Amul (Success) | Kingfisher Airlines (Failure) |
  | Organisation | Gujarat Cooperative Milk Marketing Federation (GCMMF), established 1946 | Kingfisher Airlines, founded 2005, collapsed 2012 |
  | Scale | 3.6 million farmers, 18,000+ village societies, 18 district unions, 1 state federation; ₹55,000+ crore turnover (2023) | Fleet of A320s and ATRs; ₹9,000+ crore accumulated debt at collapse |
  | Departments/participants coordinated | Farmers, village societies, district unions, state federation, dealers — all coordinated through a three-tier structure | Marketing, finance, operations — worked in silos, not coordinated |
  | Coordination mechanism | Three-tier structure (village society → district union → state federation), milk collection times, tanker schedules, 24-hour payment cycle, shared Amul brand | No structural coordination mechanism — no cross-functional committee, no integrated business plan, no shared dashboard |
  | Outcome | India's largest FMCG brand by revenue; world's largest milk producer; Operation Flood (1970-1996) built on this model | Operating licence suspended by DGCA in October 2012; employees unpaid; vendors unsettled; passengers stranded; airline ceased operations |
  | Lesson for managers | Competence at each tier plus a structural coordination mechanism produces extraordinary results from ordinary participants | Competent departments without coordination produce failure; individual excellence cannot substitute for aligned effort |

  Five-Point Coordination Checklist (one yes/no question per management function):
  1. Planning — "Do all departments share the same set of organisational objectives, and has each department's plan been checked for consistency with the others?" (Yes = coordinated; No = planning is siloed.)
  2. Organising — "Is there a clear scalar chain linking every department to a common authority, with no gaps or overlapping jurisdictions?" (Yes = coordinated; No = organising has structural gaps.)
  3. Staffing — "Are hiring plans in each department aligned with the organisation's overall growth plan, so that people are available when needed and not idle when not needed?" (Yes = coordinated; No = staffing is reactive, not aligned.)
  4. Directing — "Do supervisors in different departments communicate regularly so that individual efforts across departments are sequenced and aligned, not conflicting?" (Yes = coordinated; No = directing is departmental, not cross-functional.)
  5. Controlling — "Are performance reports shared across departments so that a deviation in one (e.g., production shortfall) triggers a coordinated response in others (e.g., sales plan adjustment)?" (Yes = coordinated; No = controlling is isolated, not integrated.)
  If any answer is "No," the organisation has a coordination gap in that function and the manager should address it before it produces a Kingfisher-style failure.
